> Let's work together on our first feature, which is Decay + superposition. The first step in this project is to design our business logic for our exponential decay function. Instead of writing the code, let's work together to design the actual exponential decay function and document it in our documentation catalog. We will identify our mathematical function, justify it, and define any constants we will start with. The standard function for exponential decay is y=C^(k*t) where y is the remaining amount after time t, C is the amount we started with, k is the rate of decay, and t is time. 1	# AI Interaction Log
2	
3	> **Status: DRAFT.** Review the Rules section and adjust it to match how the team wants to log AI use.
4	
5	This file is the timestamped record of every interaction with AI on the Half-Life project, from its first session onward.
6	
7	## Rules
8	
9	1. **Scope: the entire project.** Log every interaction with an AI tool about Half-Life, whether or not it changes a file. That includes:
10	   - generating code, tests, documentation, or configuration;
11	   - code review and debugging;
12	   - planning and design discussions;
13	   - questions and explanations.
14	
15	   This applies to every contributor and every AI tool: coding agents, chat assistants, and IDE assistants.
16	2. **One entry per task.** A task is one request to an AI tool plus the follow-up interaction needed to complete it. A session with several unrelated requests gets several entries. Inline code-completion tools (e.g. Xcode predictive code completion) are logged once per working session in which they were used, not once per suggestion.
17	3. **Timestamps.**
18	   1. Every entry records when its task started and ended.
19	   2. Its **Interactions** timeline gives the time of each human prompt or decision.
20	   3. Timestamps are local time with UTC offset in the form `YYYY-MM-DD HH:MM ±hhmm`, exactly what `date '+%Y-%m-%d %H:%M %z'` prints. Within the Interactions timeline, `HH:MM` is enough while the date is unchanged.
21	   4. Take timestamps from the clock at the time, never from memory afterwards. If a time has to be reconstructed, prefix it with `~` and say what it was reconstructed from.
22	4. **Chronological and append-only.** Append entries at the bottom of the Log section, in order of start time. An entry may be revised while its task's changes are uncommitted. Once committed, it is never edited or deleted. To correct a committed entry, add a new entry that references it.
23	5. **When to log.** Write the entry before the task is reported as finished. If the task changed files, commit the entry together with those changes. Otherwise commit it on its own.
24	6. **Honesty.** Record what the AI actually did, including incorrect answers, rejected suggestions, withdrawn work, and outputs that needed significant human correction.
25	7. **No sensitive data.** Never paste secrets, credentials, personal data, health data, or customer data into an entry. Summarize prompts instead of copying them verbatim when they contain such data.
26	8. **Accountability.** Every entry names the human responsible for the interaction and for reviewing and accepting its outcome.
27

1	# Architecture
2	
3	How Half-Life's features, use cases, repositories, and data fit together.
4	
5	## Overview
6	
7	Half-Life combines The Composable Architecture (TCA) with Clean Architecture (constitution Article I). The code is split into three layers, and dependencies always point inward, toward Domain.
8	
9	| Layer | Contains | May depend on |
10	|-------|----------|---------------|
11	| Presentation | TCA features: reducers and views | Domain |
12	| Domain | Entities, single-purpose use cases, repository protocols | Nothing |
13	| Data | Repository implementations and data sources | Domain |
14	
15	Data flows in one direction:
16	
17	```
18	 Command:  View ─▶ Action ─▶ Reducer ─▶ Effect ─▶ UseCase ─▶ Repository ─▶ DataSource
19	 Update:   Repository ─▶ AsyncStream ─▶ Observe…UseCase ─▶ Action ─▶ Reducer ─▶ State ─▶ View
20	```
21	
22	Repositories are the single source of truth. A feature never writes repository-owned data into its `State` directly. It sends a command through a use case, and the new data arrives back through its observation.
23	
24	> Important: One decision is still open. The app target defaults to `MainActor` isolation (`SWIFT_DEFAULT_ACTOR_ISOLATION = MainActor`), and TCA reducers crash under that setting. Annotating TCA types `nonisolated` leaves compiler warnings, while a `nonisolated` target default works cleanly. The patterns below assume this has been settled. Settle it before building the first feature, then update this note.
25	
26	### Lifetimes
27	
28	| Kind | Lifetime | Holds state? |
29	|------|----------|--------------|
30	| Repository | App: created once, outlives every feature | Yes, the source of truth for its data |
31	| Use case | The owning feature | No, only references to repositories |
32	| Feature `State` | The feature's presentation | Yes, a reduced projection of repository data plus UI state |
33	| Observation effect | The feature's view, via `.task { await store.send(.task).finish() }` | No |
34	
35	## Folder structure
36	
37	```
38	Half-Life/
39	├── App/                     App entry point and root feature
40	├── Features/<Feature>/      <Feature>Feature.swift (reducer), <Feature>View.swift
````

```sh
python3 - <<'EOF'
import math
T=5.5
k=math.log(2)/T
print("k per hour", k)
print("k per second", k/3600, "T1/2 seconds", T*3600)
f=lambda t,T=T: 2**(-t/T)
for t in [1,2,3,4,5.5,7,8,11,16.5,24,55]:
    print(f"t={t:5} h  frac={f(t):.4f}  of200={200*f(t):.1f}")
print("brief 200mg 4pm->11pm (7h):", 200*f(7))
for TT in [5,5.5,6]:
    print("T",TT,"200mg after 7h", 200*f(7,TT))
# implied half-life for 80mg exactly
print("implied T for 80mg at 7h:", 7/math.log2(200/80))
# superposition example: 128 @ 8:00, 64 @ 10:45, 205 @ 15:00 ; evaluate 11:00, 15:00, 23:00
doses=[(8.0,128),(10.75,64),(15.0,205)]
def A(t): return sum(D*f(t-ti) for ti,D in doses if t>=ti)
for t in [7.99,8.0,10.75,12.0,15.0,23.0]:
    print(f"A({t})={A(t):.1f}", [round(D*f(t-ti),1) for ti,D in doses if t>=ti])
# after last dose single exponential check
print("A(23) via A(15)*f(8):", A(15.0)*f(8))
# time to fall below 50 from A(15)
print("hours after 15:00 to reach 50mg:", T*math.log2(A(15.0)/50), "clock", 15+T*math.log2(A(15.0)/50))
EOF
```

````
# Caffeine Decay Model

How Half-Life calculates the caffeine in your body at any moment, from the drinks you've logged.

## Overview

Every caffeine figure in the app comes from one function: the amount of caffeine in the body at time `t`. That includes the "in your system now" number, the decay curve, and the level at bedtime. This article defines that function, explains why it was chosen, and sets its starting constants.

The model makes three choices:

1. **First-order elimination.** The body clears a fixed *fraction* of the caffeine present each hour, not a fixed amount. This gives exponential decay.
2. **Instant absorption.** A drink's whole dose enters the body at the moment it's logged.
3. **Superposition.** The caffeine from several drinks is the sum of each drink's own decay curve.

The model has a single parameter, the **half-life** (`T½`). It starts at 5.5 hours for everyone and can later be tuned per user (see <doc:CaffeineDecayModel#Tuning-the-half-life>).

## The decay function

### One drink

A drink of `D` milligrams logged at time `t₀` leaves this amount in the body at time `t`:

```
A(t) = D · e^(−k · (t − t₀))    when t ≥ t₀
A(t) = 0                        when t < t₀

k = ln 2 / T½
```

This can also be written in terms of the half-life, and the two forms are mathematically identical:

```
A(t) = D · 2^(−(t − t₀) / T½)
```

Each time `T½` passes, the amount halves.

| Symbol | Meaning | Unit |
|--------|---------|------|
| `A(t)` | Caffeine remaining in the body at time `t` | mg |
| `D` | Caffeine in the drink (dose) | mg |
| `t₀` | Time the drink was logged | time |
| `t` | Time being evaluated | time |
| `T½` | Elimination half-life | hours |
| `k` | Elimination rate constant | per hour |

### Several drinks: superposition

The total at time `t` is the sum of every drink logged at or before `t`:

```
A(t) =    Σ     Dᵢ · e^(−k · (tᵢ … see below))
```

More precisely:

```
A(t) = Σ  Dᵢ · e^(−k · (t − tᵢ))
      i : tᵢ ≤ t
```

Drinks logged after `t` add nothing to `A(t)`.

Superposition is valid because first-order elimination is **linear**. The fraction cleared each hour doesn't depend on how much caffeine is present, so drinks don't interact. Each one decays on its own curve, and the total is their sum. The decay curve therefore jumps up by `Dᵢ` at each drink, then decays smoothly until the next drink.

### After the last drink, the total is a single exponential

Every term shares the same `k`. So from any time `s` at or after the last drink, the whole total decays like one dose of size `A(s)`:

```
A(t) = A(s) · e^(−k · (t − s))      for any s ≥ t_last, t ≥ s
```

This means the app's central question, "*when* will I be below `X` mg?", has an exact answer once the day's drinks are logged:

```
t = s + T½ · log₂(A(s) / X)         when A(s) > X
```

## Constants

| Constant | Starting value | Notes |
|----------|----------------|-------|
| Half-life `T½` | **5.5 hours** (19,800 s) | The midpoint of the brief's "roughly 5–6 hours". It's the model's only parameter. |
| Rate constant `k` | ln 2 / 5.5 h ≈ **0.1260 h⁻¹** (≈ 3.5007 × 10⁻⁵ s⁻¹) | Always derived from `T½`, never set or stored separately. |

Amounts are in milligrams. This article uses hours to keep the numbers readable. In code, times are Foundation `Date` values and durations are `TimeInterval` seconds, so the default half-life is 19,800 seconds.

## Why this model

- **It matches how caffeine is cleared.** At everyday doses, the liver clears caffeine at a rate proportional to how much is present, so a fixed fraction goes each hour. That is first-order elimination, and first-order elimination produces exponential decay.
- **The half-life doesn't depend on the dose.** Under first-order elimination, a 100 mg cup and a 400 mg cup both halve in the same 5.5 hours. The brief's "200 mg" is the size of its example, not an input to the half-life.
- **It reproduces the brief.** 200 mg at 4:00pm leaves 82.8 mg at 11:00pm. The brief says "80 mg", which corresponds to a half-life of about 5.3 hours, well within its 5–6 hour range.
- **It's parameterized by the half-life.** The half-life is the number people understand and the literature reports, and it's the value the app will tune per user. `k` is derived from it, so there's a single source of truth.
- **It's the simplest model that answers the question** (constitution Article III.1). It has one parameter and one closed-form expression, and it's cheap enough to evaluate at every point along the curve.

### How much the half-life matters

The brief's own range already moves the bedtime figure. Here is 200 mg at 4:00pm, evaluated at 11:00pm:

| Half-life | Remaining at 11:00pm |
|-----------|----------------------|
| 5 hours | 75.8 mg |
| 5.5 hours | 82.8 mg |
| 6 hours | 89.1 mg |

Real people differ far more than that. Half-lives vary several-fold between individuals. Smoking shortens the half-life, while pregnancy, oral contraceptives, and liver impairment lengthen it. That's why the half-life has to be tunable.

## Assumptions and limitations

The app must not claim more precision than this model has (the brief's *Honesty* criterion). Its known simplifications:

- **Instant absorption.** In reality, caffeine is absorbed over time and peaks somewhere between about 30 minutes and 2 hours after drinking. As a result, the model overstates caffeine for roughly the first hour after each drink, and slightly understates it afterwards. Bateman absorption (roadmap rank 5) replaces this assumption.
- **Complete absorption.** The whole labelled dose is assumed to reach the bloodstream. Caffeine taken by mouth is almost completely absorbed, so this is a small error. The larger uncertainty is how accurate the drink's caffeine estimate is in the first place.
- **Linear kinetics at every dose.** At very high intakes, elimination can slow down, which lengthens the effective half-life. The model ignores this.
- **A constant half-life.** The model applies one half-life at every hour of every day. It ignores changes from medication, illness, pregnancy, or time of day.
- **Amount, not concentration.** `A(t)` is milligrams in the whole body, not blood concentration. Using the amount avoids asking for body weight, but the same number of milligrams has a stronger effect on a smaller person.
- **Caffeine only.** The body converts caffeine into other compounds, mainly paraxanthine, and some of them are also stimulants. The model tracks caffeine alone.

## Rules at the edges

- A drink contributes nothing before its logged time, and its full dose exactly at its logged time.
- A dose is never negative, and the half-life is always greater than zero. Inputs are validated before they reach the model.
- Mathematically, a drink never reaches exactly zero. After 10 half-lives (55 hours at the default), less than 0.1% of it remains.

## Worked examples

These values were computed from the formulas above with `T½ = 5.5 h`. They're the reference cases for the model's first unit tests.

### One 200 mg drink

| Hours since drinking | Fraction remaining | Remaining |
|---------------------:|-------------------:|----------:|
| 0 | 1.0000 | 200.0 mg |
| 1 | 0.8816 | 176.3 mg |
| 2 | 0.7772 | 155.4 mg |
| 4 | 0.6040 | 120.8 mg |
| 5.5 | 0.5000 | 100.0 mg |
| 7 | 0.4139 | 82.8 mg |
| 11 | 0.2500 | 50.0 mg |
| 16.5 | 0.1250 | 25.0 mg |
| 24 | 0.0486 | 9.7 mg |

### A day of drinks

The drinks: 128 mg at 8:00am, 64 mg at 10:45am, and 205 mg at 3:00pm.

| Time | From 8:00am | From 10:45am | From 3:00pm | Total |
|------|------------:|-------------:|------------:|------:|
| 7:59am | — | — | — | 0.0 mg |
| 8:00am | 128.0 | — | — | 128.0 mg |
| 10:45am | 90.5 | 64.0 | — | 154.5 mg |
| 12:00pm | 77.3 | 54.7 | — | 132.0 mg |
| 3:00pm | 53.0 | 37.5 | 205.0 | 295.4 mg |
| 11:00pm | 19.3 | 13.7 | 74.8 | 107.8 mg |

Two checks on the single-exponential property:

- The 11:00pm total also equals `295.4 · 2^(−8 / 5.5)` = 107.8 mg, calculated from the 3:00pm total alone.
- The total falls below 50 mg at `3:00pm + 5.5 h · log₂(295.4 / 50)` ≈ 3:00pm + 14.1 h, which is about **5:06am** the next morning.

## Tuning the half-life

The half-life is the model's only per-user parameter. Everyone starts at 5.5 hours. Later features refine it:

- The onboarding survey (roadmap rank 6) sets an informed starting value.
- The personal half-life estimator (rank 9) fits it to the user's own data.
- Settings (rank 21) lets the user override it.

Those features will decide the allowed range and how a changed half-life is stored. The function itself doesn't change: it takes the half-life as an input.

### 20:58:53 · Claude

I've written the design as a new DocC article, `Half-Life/Documentation.docc/CaffeineDecayModel.md`, and linked it from the catalog's landing page. It uses a 5.5-**hour** half-life rather than the 5.5 days you wrote. The brief says 5–6 hours, and its own example only works in hours: with a 5.5-day half-life, 200 mg at 4pm would still be about 192 mg at 11pm instead of about 80 mg. Please confirm.

**The formula.** The standard decay form is `y = C · e^(−k·t)`, not `C^(k·t)`: C multiplies the exponential, and the minus sign is what makes it decay. For one drink of D mg drunk at time t₀:

```
A(t) = D · e^(−k·(t − t₀))   for t ≥ t₀, and 0 before it
k    = ln 2 / T½              (same curve as D · 2^(−(t − t₀)/T½))
```

For several drinks, the total at any moment is the sum of each drink's own curve, counting only drinks already logged by then.

**Why this function**
- **It matches how the body clears caffeine.** The liver removes a fixed fraction each hour, not a fixed amount, which is exactly what exponential decay describes.
- **Adding up the drinks is valid.** Each drink decays on its own, whatever else is in your system, so the total is just their sum.
- **The dose doesn't change the half-life.** A 100 mg cup and a 400 mg cup both halve in 5.5 hours, so the "200 mg" in the brief is only the size of its example.
- **It gives an exact answer to "when".** After the last drink of the day, the total decays as if it were a single dose, so "when will I be below X mg?" has a one-line answer: `t = s + T½ · log₂(A(s)/X)`, where s is any time after the last drink. That's the question the whole app is built around.
- **It checks out against the brief.** 200 mg at 4pm leaves 82.8 mg at 11pm, close to the brief's "80 mg".

**Constants**

| Constant | Value |
|---|---|
| Half-life (T½) | 5.5 h (19,800 s). This is the only setting, and the one users will tune later. |
| Decay rate (k) | ln 2 / 5.5 ≈ 0.1260 per hour. Always calculated from the half-life, never stored separately. |

**What else the article covers**
- A table showing how much the answer moves within the brief's own 5–6 hour range: 75.8 to 89.1 mg at 11pm.
- A limitations section, since the brief grades honesty. The biggest one is that each drink counts in full the moment it's logged; the Bateman absorption item (roadmap rank 5) replaces that.
- Rules for edge cases.
- Worked examples, computed by script rather than by hand, to use as the first unit tests. One is a three-drink day that comes to 107.8 mg at 11pm and drops below 50 mg around 5:06am.

The documentation build succeeded with zero warnings, and the new article is in the output. No code changed, so I didn't re-run tests or coverage. I added the `ai_log.md` entry, and nothing is committed yet.

**Decisions for you**
1. **Hours:** please confirm you meant 5.5 hours.
2. **Sources:** some physiology in the article has no citations yet: how long caffeine takes to peak, what lengthens or shortens the half-life, slower clearance at very high doses, and paraxanthine. Should I find sources, or cut those lines back?
3. **Where the code lives:** should the function be a plain Domain function on the entities, or its own use case (e.g. `CalculateCaffeineLevelUseCase`)? We'll need this before the TDD cycle.
4. **Actor isolation:** the TCA actor-isolation setting from `CLAUDE.md` is still undecided. The math itself doesn't need TCA, but the first feature that shows it will.

- `new_string`:

```
Amounts are in milligrams. This article uses hours to keep the numbers readable. In code, times are Foundation `Date` values and durations are `TimeInterval` seconds, so the default half-life is 19,800 seconds.

## Entities

The model reads two Domain entities and produces a third. Each is a plain `Sendable`, `Equatable` value type with no framework dependencies (constitution Article I.10). The entities describe what goes into the model and what comes out of it, not how the curve is calculated. Switching to Bateman absorption changes the calculation and leaves them alone.

### CaffeineIntake

One drink's caffeine entering the body. It's `Identifiable`.

| Property | Type | Meaning |
|----------|------|---------|
| `id` | `UUID` | Identifies the intake, so it can be listed, edited, or deleted. |
| `milligrams` | `Double` | The dose `D`. Always greater than zero. |
| `consumedAt` | `Date` | The time `t₀` the drink was consumed. |

An intake records when the drink was *consumed*, not when it was logged. The two differ whenever someone logs after drinking, as with the composer's "1h ago" option or retroactive timing (roadmap rank 17). The model needs the moment the caffeine entered the body.

### CaffeineHalfLife

The user's elimination half-life `T½`.

| Property | Type | Meaning |
|----------|------|---------|
| `seconds` | `TimeInterval` | The half-life. Always greater than zero. |

- A `standard` value of 19,800 s (5.5 h) applies until the user's own half-life is known.
- The elimination rate `k` is derived from it as `ln 2 / seconds`, and is never stored.

This is a dedicated type rather than a bare `TimeInterval`. That keeps the greater-than-zero rule and the derivation of `k` in one place, and gives the tuning features (roadmap ranks 6, 9, and 21) a single value to produce.

### CaffeineLevel

The model's output: the caffeine in the body at one moment.

| Property | Type | Meaning |
|----------|------|---------|
| `date` | `Date` | The time `t` being evaluated. |
| `milligrams` | `Double` | `A(t)`. Never negative. |

A curve is an array of levels in time order. The Today screen's "in your system now" figure is a single level, and its chart is a curve.

### What Bateman absorption changes

| Entity | Instant absorption | Bateman absorption |
|--------|--------------------|--------------------|
| `CaffeineIntake` | Dose and time consumed | Unchanged |
| `CaffeineHalfLife` | Elimination half-life | Unchanged: the elimination half-life means the same thing in both models |
| `CaffeineLevel` | Milligrams at a moment | Unchanged |

Bateman absorption adds an absorption rate constant. These entities stay the same as long as that rate is a single constant of the model. If the Bateman work decides that absorption varies by drink or by person, it adds a new value: a property on `CaffeineIntake` for a per-drink rate, or a new entity for a per-person rate. That change would update this section.

### Units

Amounts are `Double` milligrams and durations are `TimeInterval` seconds, with the unit in each property's name. The model is pure arithmetic on these values, and `Date` arithmetic already uses `TimeInterval`. Wrapping the values in `Measurement` would add a conversion to every calculation and gain nothing inside the Domain. Presentation converts to `Measurement` when it formats a value, so everything users see stays locale-aware (constitution Article VII.3).

```

- `new_string`:

```
Presentation converts to `Measurement` when it formats a value, so everything users see stays locale-aware (constitution Article VII.3).

## Where the calculation lives

The decay function lives in the Domain layer as a **business rule**. That's a stateless type, working name `CaffeineDecayRule`, that turns intakes and a half-life into caffeine levels. It depends only on the entities above and on Foundation.

- **The repository executes the rule.** When the repository's intakes change, it applies the rule and publishes the resulting curve on its `AsyncStream`.
- **Features observe the curve.** An `Observe…` use case streams the curve to features (constitution Article I.4). There's no use case for the calculation itself: nothing asks for a calculation on its own, and features only need the curve.
- **Bateman absorption replaces the rule's implementation.** The repository, the use case, and the entities keep working without changes.

The rule has two operations:

| Operation | Takes | Returns |
|-----------|-------|---------|
| Level | Intakes, a half-life, and a moment `t` | The `CaffeineLevel` at `t` |
| Curve | Intakes, a half-life, and a series of moments | `[CaffeineLevel]`, one level per moment |

Still to decide:

- **Which moments the curve covers, and how far apart they are.** "Now" keeps moving between emissions, so the "in your system now" figure can't come from a curve that's calculated only when intakes change.
- **Which repository owns the curve, and how it gets the half-life** that the tuning features will store.

```

Repositories are the single source of truth. A feature never writes repository-owned data into its `State` directly. It sends a command through a use case, and the new data arrives back through its observation.

- `new_string`:

```
Repositories are the single source of truth. A feature never writes repository-owned data into its `State` directly. It sends a command through a use case, and the new data arrives back through its observation.

A repository can execute a Domain business rule to derive what it publishes from the data it holds. For example, it can turn caffeine intakes into a decay curve before the curve goes out on its `AsyncStream`.

```

### 21:09:05 · Claude

I've added the entity design and the business-rule placement to `CaffeineDecayModel.md`, and put business rules into `Architecture.md` as a new part of the Domain layer. The documentation build succeeded with zero warnings. No code changed, so there were no tests to run, and nothing is committed yet.

**Entities** (Domain layer, plain `Sendable`, `Equatable` value types):

| Entity | Fields | Role |
|---|---|---|
| `CaffeineIntake` | `id`, `milligrams`, `consumedAt` | One drink going in (the dose and when it was drunk) |
| `CaffeineHalfLife` | `seconds`, plus a `standard` value of 19,800 s | The half-life, the one setting users will tune. The decay rate `k` is always calculated from it, never stored. |
| `CaffeineLevel` | `date`, `milligrams` | The output: caffeine in the body at one moment. A curve is a list of these. |

- **Consumed, not logged:** the model now uses the time the drink was consumed everywhere, not the time it was logged. Anyone who logs after drinking (the composer's "1h ago") makes those two times differ.
- **Plain numbers:** amounts are stored as plain numbers (`Double` milligrams, `TimeInterval` seconds). They're converted to `Measurement` only when shown on screen, so what users see still follows their locale.

**Does Bateman change the entities?** Mostly not. Both models take the same inputs and produce the same output. The one exception: Bateman adds an absorption rate. If that's a single fixed number, nothing changes. If you later want it to vary by drink (a gum versus a coffee) or by person, that adds a field to `CaffeineIntake` or a new entity. The article says this explicitly.

Bateman also removes one convenience. With instant absorption, "when will I be below X mg?" has an exact formula. With Bateman it has to be solved numerically, though it's close to the formula again once the last drink is fully absorbed. I've noted that in the article.

**Where the calculation lives:** as you described. `CaffeineDecayRule` (a working name) is a stateless Domain business rule with two operations: the level at one moment, and a curve over a series of moments. The repository runs it when its intakes change and publishes the curve. An `Observe…` use case streams the curve to features, and there's no use case for the calculation itself. When Bateman lands, only the rule's implementation changes.

**Decisions for you**
1. **Constitution:** Article I's Domain row lists entities, use cases, and repository protocols, but not business rules. The catalog now documents them, but I haven't touched the constitution. Do you want an amendment?
2. **How far the curve reaches, and how often it updates:** if the repository recalculates only when intakes change, the "in your system now" figure goes stale as time passes. We need to decide what time range the curve covers and how finely it's sampled.
3. **Who owns the curve and the half-life:** which repository publishes the curve, and where does it get the half-life once tuning exists?
4. **Names:** `CaffeineIntake`, `CaffeineHalfLife`, `CaffeineLevel`, and `CaffeineDecayRule` are working names. The constitution and the Architecture article use `CaffeineEntry` as their example entity name, so tell me if you'd rather use that.

**A stray edit:** in the article's worked example, the line `3:00pm + 5.5 h · log₂(295.4 / 50)` lost its `·` in an edit made outside this session. It now reads `5.5 h  log₂…`. I left it alone in case it was deliberate. If it was accidental, I can put it back.

I added a new `ai_log.md` entry for this task. I also updated the earlier decay-function entry with your 5.5-hour confirmation and the deferred sources, and marked its code-placement question as resolved. Actor isolation remains deferred.

### 21:18:16 · Claude

`CaffeineIntake` is settled, and the absorption rate will be its own entity when we get to Bateman. I'll record both in the article.

Two points on dropping curves before I write the rule:

- **"Negligible", not "statistically insignificant".** The model is deterministic, so no statistical test is involved. What we mean is "below the model's own precision", and that's easy to defend. The brief's 5–6 hour range alone moves the bedtime figure by ±7 mg, so a 1 mg tail is noise. The brief grades confidence language, so I'd use "negligible" in the docs.
- **The rule has to survive Bateman.** Under Bateman, a new drink starts at 0 mg and rises. A plain "below the threshold, drop it" rule would discard every drink the moment it's logged. So the rule should drop a drink only once it's past its peak and below the threshold. Under instant absorption the peak is the moment of drinking, so this changes nothing today.

The threshold itself is a constant, like the 5.5 hours, so I'd like your call on it:

```
The model makes three choices:

1. **First-order elimination.** The body clears a fixed *fraction* of the caffeine present each hour, not a fixed amount. This gives exponential decay.
2. **Instant absorption.** A drink's whole dose enters the body at the moment it's consumed.
3. **Superposition.** The caffeine from several drinks is the sum of each drink's own decay curve.

The model has a single parameter, the **half-life** (`T½`).
```
- `new_string`:

```
The model makes four choices:

1. **First-order elimination.** The body clears a fixed *fraction* of the caffeine present each hour, not a fixed amount. This gives exponential decay.
2. **Instant absorption.** A drink's whole dose enters the body at the moment it's consumed.
3. **Superposition.** The caffeine from several drinks is the sum of each drink's own decay curve.
4. **Negligible intakes are dropped.** Once a drink's remaining caffeine falls below 0.5 mg, it stops counting.

The model has one per-user parameter, the **half-life** (`T½`).
```

**21:20:42 · Tool call: Edit**

- `new_string`:

````
The total returns to a single exponential, to a very close approximation, once the last drink has been fully absorbed.

### Dropping negligible intakes

Mathematically, a drink never reaches zero. The rule stops counting a drink once its remaining caffeine is **negligible**, which means below `ε` = 0.5 mg:

```
A(t) = Σ  Dᵢ · e^(−k · (t − tᵢ))
      i : tᵢ ≤ t  and  Dᵢ · e^(−k · (t − tᵢ)) ≥ ε
```

Under instant absorption, an intake counts from the moment it's consumed until this time, and never again afterwards:

```
tᵢ + T½ · log₂(Dᵢ / ε)
```

"Negligible" means the amount is below the model's own precision. It isn't a statistical test, because the model is deterministic. The threshold of 0.5 mg:

- **Is below what the display shows.** Rounded to whole milligrams, 0.5 mg shows as 0 mg, so a drink dropped on its own always reads 0 mg.
- **Is far below the model's uncertainty.** The brief's 5–6 hour range alone moves the bedtime figure by about ±7 mg.

Dropping intakes has these effects:

- **The total steps down by less than 0.5 mg when an intake is dropped.** If other intakes are still counting, the step can make the rounded total tick down by 1 mg slightly early.
- **The error stays small.** At any moment, the curve understates the true total by the sum of the dropped tails. Each tail was below 0.5 mg when it was dropped, and keeps shrinking.
- **The work is bounded.** Every intake drops out within `T½ · log₂(D / ε)`. At the default half-life, that's under 2 days for a 200 mg drink and about 2.5 days even for 1,000 mg, so the repository never needs to evaluate old intakes. The window grows with the half-life: at 10 hours, 1,000 mg takes about 4.6 days.
- **The closed-form "when" answer can be slightly off.** It can miss by up to 0.5 mg for each intake dropped between `s` and `t`. That matters only for targets `X` of a few milligrams.
- **An intake of less than 0.5 mg never counts.** Real drinks are well above this.

The rule has to survive Bateman absorption. Under Bateman, a drink starts at 0 mg and rises to a peak, so "below `ε`" on its own would drop every new drink the moment it's logged. The rule is therefore: **an intake is dropped once it is past its peak and below `ε`**. Under instant absorption, the peak is the moment the drink is consumed, so this is the same rule.

````

### When intakes drop out

| Dose | Stops counting after |
|-----:|---------------------:|

### 21:22:40 · Claude

The rule for dropping negligible intakes is now in `CaffeineDecayModel.md`, using your 0.5 mg threshold. The article also records that `CaffeineIntake` carries no drink details and that the absorption rate will be its own entity when we build Bateman. The documentation build succeeded with no warnings from our catalog. No code changed, so there were no tests to run, and nothing is committed.

**The rule:** an intake stops counting once it's **past its peak and below 0.5 mg**.
- Under instant absorption the peak is the moment of drinking. So each intake counts until `tᵢ + T½ · log₂(Dᵢ / 0.5 mg)` and never again after that.
- The "past its peak" part matters later. Under Bateman a new drink starts at 0 mg, and a plain "below 0.5 mg" rule would drop every drink the moment it's logged.
- 0.5 mg is a fixed constant, the same for everyone, alongside the 5.5-hour half-life. The docs call it "negligible" rather than "statistically insignificant", since the model has no statistics in it.

**What it means in practice:**
- **Drop-out times:** 64 mg stops counting after 38.5 h, 200 mg after 47.5 h, and 400 mg after 53 h.
- **Small steps:** when an intake drops out, the total falls by less than 0.5 mg. A drink dropped on its own always reads 0 mg. If other drinks are still counting, the rounded total can tick down 1 mg a little early. That corrects what I told you when I offered the options.
- **Bounded work:** even 1,000 mg is gone within about 2.5 days at 5.5 h, so the repository never has to evaluate old intakes. That window grows with the half-life: about 4.6 days at 10 h.
- **"When below X" answers:** these can be off by up to 0.5 mg for each intake that drops out in between. That only matters for targets of a few milligrams.
- **A test case for later:** 128 mg is exactly 0.5 mg after 44 h (8 half-lives). It still counts at 44 h and drops out right after.

**Still open:**
1. **Curve timing:** what time range the curve covers and how often it's sampled. "Now" moves between updates, so the "in your system now" figure goes stale if the curve is only recalculated when intakes change.
2. **Ownership:** which repository publishes the curve, and where it gets the half-life.
3. **Rounding:** I've assumed whole milligrams on screen, based on the prototype, but nothing decides that yet.
4. **The missing `·`:** the worked-example line `5.5 h  log₂(295.4 / 50)` is still missing its `·`. I've left it for you to fix or confirm.

import math
T=5.5
f=lambda t,T=T: 2**(-t/T)
print("single 200mg:")
for t in [0,1,2,4,5.5,7,11,16.5,24]:
    print(f"  {t:5} h  {f(t):.4f}  {200*f(t):.2f}")
print("sensitivity 7h:", [f"{200*f(7,x):.2f}" for x in (5,5.5,6)])
doses=[(8.0,128),(10.75,64),(15.0,205)]
print("day table:")
for t in [7+59/60,8.0,10.75,12.0,15.0,23.0]:
    parts=[D*f(t-ti) for ti,D in doses if t>=ti]
    print(f"  t={t:6.3f}", [f"{p:.2f}" for p in parts], f"total {sum(parts):.2f}")
A15=sum(D*f(15-ti) for ti,D in doses)
print("A15", A15, "A23 via A15", A15*f(8))
h=T*math.log2(A15/50); print("hours to 50:", h, "clock", 15+h, "min", (h%1)*60)
print("eps visibility: 200mg drop at 0.005mg:", T*math.log2(200/0.005))
EOF
```

```
"Negligible" means the amount is below the model's own precision. It isn't a statistical test, because the model is deterministic. The threshold of 0.5 mg:

- **Is below what the display shows.** Rounded to whole milligrams, 0.5 mg shows as 0 mg, so a drink dropped on its own always reads 0 mg.
- **Is far below the model's uncertainty.** The brief's 5–6 hour range alone moves the bedtime figure by about ±7 mg.

Dropping intakes has these effects:

- **The total steps down by less than 0.5 mg when an intake is dropped.** If other intakes are still counting, the step can make the rounded total tick down by 1 mg slightly early.
```
- `new_string`:

```
"Negligible" means the amount is below the model's own precision. It isn't a statistical test, because the model is deterministic. The brief's 5–6 hour range alone moves the bedtime figure by about ±7 mg, so a remainder under 0.5 mg is well inside the model's error.

Dropping intakes has these effects:

- **The total steps down by less than 0.5 mg when an intake is dropped.** At two decimal places, the step is visible. For example, a drink that's the only one still counting goes from 0.50 mg to 0.00 mg.
```

- `old_string`:

```
- **The repository executes the rule.** When the repository's intakes change, it applies the rule and publishes the resulting curve on its `AsyncStream`.
- **Features observe the curve.** An `Observe…` use case streams the curve to features (constitution Article I.4). There's no use case for the calculation itself: nothing asks for a calculation on its own, and features only need the curve.
- **Bateman absorption replaces the rule's implementation.** The repository, the use case, and the entities keep working without changes.

The rule has two operations:

| Operation | Takes | Returns |
|-----------|-------|---------|
| Level | Intakes, a half-life, and a moment `t` | The `CaffeineLevel` at `t` |
| Curve | Intakes, a half-life, and a series of moments | `[CaffeineLevel]`, one level per moment |

Both operations leave out negligible intakes (see "Dropping negligible intakes" above).

Still to decide:

- **Which moments the curve covers, and how far apart they are.** "Now" keeps moving between emissions, so the "in your system now" figure can't come from a curve that's calculated only when intakes change.
- **Which repository owns the curve, and how it gets the half-life** that the tuning features will store.

```
- `new_string`:

```
- **`CaffeineDecayRepository` executes the rule.** It fetches the intakes that aren't marked negligible from its data source, applies the rule, and publishes the entire curve on its `AsyncStream`.
- **The repository marks intakes that no longer count.** When the rule reports that intakes are negligible at the current time, the repository has the data source mark them, so later fetches skip them. Marking only saves work. From the current time onward, the curve is the same whether or not an intake has been marked yet.
- **Features observe the curve.** An `Observe…` use case streams the curve to features (constitution Article I.4). There's no use case for the calculation itself: nothing asks for a calculation on its own, and features only need the curve.
- **Presentation picks the time window.** The repository always publishes the whole curve, and each feature filters it to the window it shows. The curve runs until the last intake stops counting, so it already covers "now" and the hours ahead. A feature can read the current level from it at any moment without asking for a new curve.
- **Bateman absorption replaces the rule's implementation.** The repository, the use case, and the entities keep working without changes.

The rule has three operations:

| Operation | Takes | Returns |
|-----------|-------|---------|
| Level | Intakes, a half-life, and a moment `t` | The `CaffeineLevel` at `t` |
| Curve | Intakes and a half-life | `[CaffeineLevel]`, from the earliest intake until the last one stops counting |
| Negligible intakes | Intakes, a half-life, and the current time | The intakes that are negligible at that time |

The Level and Curve operations leave out negligible intakes (see "Dropping negligible intakes" above).

Once an intake is marked, it no longer appears in this curve, even for the hours when it still counted. So the curve describes the intakes that still count, not the past. Curves for past days, like the Patterns screen's, need a source that includes marked intakes.

Still to decide:

- **How far apart the curve's samples are.**
- **What clears a negligible mark.** Whether an intake is negligible depends on the half-life and on the intake's dose and time. Any of these can make a marked intake count again: tuning the half-life (roadmap ranks 6, 9, and 21), editing or backdating an intake (rank 17), or Bateman absorption.
- **How `CaffeineDecayRepository` learns about new intakes, and where it gets the half-life.**

## Testable requirements

Each requirement is written so that one test can prove it. Tests refer to requirements by their IDs.

### CaffeineDecayRule

Unit-tested directly. The rule is pure, so its tests need no fakes.

| ID | Requirement |
|----|-------------|
| RULE-1 | The level at `t` is the sum of `Dᵢ · 2^(−(t − tᵢ) / T½)` over the intakes that count at `t`. It matches every worked example below to two decimal places. |
| RULE-2 | An intake adds nothing before its `consumedAt`, and its full dose at exactly `consumedAt`. |
| RULE-3 | An intake counts while its remaining amount is at least 0.5 mg. It stops counting once it's past its peak and below 0.5 mg. For example, a 128 mg intake counts at 44 hours after `consumedAt`, and not after that. |
| RULE-4 | Given the current time, the rule reports exactly the intakes that are negligible at that time. An intake consumed after the current time is never negligible. |
| RULE-5 | The curve starts at the earliest intake's `consumedAt` and ends when the last intake stops counting. With no intakes, the curve is empty. |
| RULE-6 | The rule reads no clock and holds no state. The current time is an input, and the same inputs always give the same outputs. |

### CaffeineDecayRepository

Tested against a fake intake data source and a fixed current time.

| ID | Requirement |
|----|-------------|
| REPO-1 | It asks the data source only for intakes that aren't marked negligible. |
| REPO-2 | It publishes the entire curve on an `AsyncStream`: the current curve as soon as someone subscribes, then a new curve after every change (constitution Article I.12). |
| REPO-3 | When the rule reports intakes as negligible at the current time, it has the data source mark exactly those intakes, and no others. |
| REPO-4 | For every moment at or after the current time, the published curve is the same whether or not negligible intakes have been marked yet. |

### Intake data source

Tested in isolation.

| ID | Requirement |
|----|-------------|
| DATA-1 | It returns only intakes that aren't marked negligible. |
| DATA-2 | It marks the given intakes as negligible, and the marks persist. |
| DATA-3 | Marking never deletes an intake. The full history stays stored for other features. |

When the data source is built, the Architecture article's "Data and privacy" table lists the stored intake and its mark (constitution Article V.2).

### Presentation

Tested with an exhaustive `TestStore`, with the `Observe…` use case overridden.

| ID | Requirement |
|----|-------------|
| VIEW-1 | A feature filters the curve to its own time window. It never evaluates the decay function itself. |
| VIEW-2 | Caffeine amounts are shown to two decimal places, formatted with locale-aware APIs (constitution Article VII.3). |

```

96:- **The total steps down by less than 0.5 mg when an intake is dropped.** At two decimal places, the step is visible. For example, a drink that's the only one still counting goes from 0.50 mg to 0.00 mg.
97:- **The error stays small.** At any moment, the curve understates the true total by the sum of the dropped tails. Each tail was below 0.5 mg when it was dropped, and keeps shrinking.
99:- **The closed-form "when" answer can be slightly off.** It can miss by up to 0.5 mg for each intake dropped between `s` and `t`. That matters only for targets `X` of a few milligrams.
100:- **An intake of less than 0.5 mg never counts.** Real drinks are well above this.

331:128 mg is a boundary case. After exactly 44 hours (8 half-lives), exactly 0.5 mg remains, so the intake still counts at 44 hours and is dropped immediately after. In the day of drinks above, the 8:00am drink leaves the curve at 4:00am two days later.

### 21:31:44 · Claude

I've written up the negligible-intake flow as testable requirements in `CaffeineDecayModel.md`, set rounding to two decimal places, and fixed the worked example. `CaffeineDecayRepository` is now in the Architecture article's Repositories table, marked as designed but not built. The docs build with no warnings from our catalog. No code changed, so there were no tests to run, and nothing is committed.

**The flow as documented:**
- **Fetch:** the repository asks its data source only for intakes that aren't marked negligible.
- **Publish:** it runs the rule and publishes the entire curve. The curve runs from the earliest intake until the last one stops counting, so it already covers "now" and the hours ahead. That removes the staleness problem I raised earlier.
- **Mark:** when the rule reports an intake as negligible, the repository has the data source mark it. The mark is stored by the data source only; `CaffeineIntake` never sees it.
- **Filter:** each screen filters the curve to its own time window and never runs the decay math itself.

**Testable requirements:** there are 15, each provable by one test:

| Group | IDs | Covers |
|---|---|---|
| Rule | RULE-1 to RULE-6 | The formula, drop-out behaviour, and curve range |
| Repository | REPO-1 to REPO-4 | Fetching, publishing, and marking |
| Data source | DATA-1 to DATA-3 | Unmarked-only fetches, and marks that persist |
| Screens | VIEW-1 to VIEW-2 | Filtering, and two-decimal display |

Four of them are my additions rather than things you stated, so please check them:
- **RULE-5:** where the curve starts and ends.
- **RULE-6:** the rule reads no clock; the current time is passed in.
- **REPO-4:** marking changes nothing from the current time onward. It only saves work.
- **DATA-3:** marking never deletes an intake.

**Worked example:** the `·` is back. At two decimals, every mg figure now has two decimal places, and one value changed: the 12:00pm total is 131.99 mg, not 132.0.

**Decisions for you**
1. **Throw or return?** You said the rule "throws" when an intake is negligible. I've written "reports" instead. A negligible intake is an expected result, not an error, and a throw would stop the whole curve calculation at the first negligible intake. I'd have the rule return the negligible intakes. Is that OK?
2. **What clears a mark?** Marks are permanent, but whether an intake is negligible depends on the half-life and on its dose and time. Tuning the half-life upward (ranks 6, 9, 21), editing or backdating a drink (rank 17), or Bateman can all make a marked intake count again. We need a rule for that, for example clearing all marks whenever the half-life or an intake changes.
3. **Past-day curves.** A marked intake disappears from the curve's past too, not just its future. So past-day charts, like Patterns' 7D/30D/90D, can't come from this repository.
4. **Two decimals and honesty.** "82.78 mg" suggests hundredths-of-a-milligram precision. The half-life range alone makes the bedtime figure uncertain by about ±7 mg, and the brief grades overstatement. It also makes the 0.5 mg drop visible on screen (0.50 → 0.00). I've documented two decimals as decided. You could keep two decimals for the tests while displaying whole milligrams.
5. **Still open:** how far apart the curve's sample points are, how the repository hears about new intakes, and where it gets the half-life. Actor isolation is still deferred.

I added a new `ai_log.md` entry and updated the previous entry's notes to record what this task resolved.

### 21:36:26 · Human

> It is okay to return. I agree with the semantic of throwing being errors. As for clearing a mark, it's more setting a state on the data source's data structure. We shouldn't need to clear a mark. The data source will filter it's query based on what has been "marked" as negligible. For past-day curves, that will be a separate path to the data source we can handle separately. For the purposes of our currently active curve, we will filter out negligible curvers. We'll keep two decimals in case we get more accurate data, but presentation layer can always display rounded data.

Once an intake is marked, it no longer appears in this curve, even for the hours when it still counted. So the curve describes the intakes that still count, not the past. Curves for past days, like the Patterns screen's, need a source that includes marked intakes.

Still to decide:

- **How far apart the curve's samples are.**
- **What clears a negligible mark.** Whether an intake is negligible depends on the half-life and on the intake's dose and time. Any of these can make a marked intake count again: tuning the half-life (roadmap ranks 6, 9, and 21), editing or backdating an intake (rank 17), or Bateman absorption.

- `new_string`:

```
The repository publishes the **active curve**. Once an intake is marked, it no longer appears in that curve, even for the hours when it still counted, so the active curve describes the intakes that still count, not the past. Past-day curves, like the Patterns screen's, will read intakes through a separate path to the data source that includes marked ones. That path is designed separately.

Because marks are never cleared, a marked intake stays out of the active curve even when a later change would make it count again. The main case is a longer tuned half-life. The error this causes is small. At the default half-life, a 200 mg intake is marked 47.54 hours after it's consumed. At that moment it would still hold 0.82 mg under a 6-hour half-life, 1.81 mg under 7 hours, or 3.25 mg under 8 hours, and those amounts keep shrinking.

Still to decide:

- **How far apart the curve's samples are.**
- **How `CaffeineDecayRepository` learns about new intakes, and where it gets the half-life.**
```

### 21:37:37 · Human (sent mid-turn)

> As for the curve itself. As a user, I'm likely looking for the next few hours, so we should test out minute-level precision here. I know that will create a massive number of data points, so we may change that in the future.

**21:38:33 · Tool call: Bash** — Timestamp and count minute-level curve points

```sh
date '+%Y-%m-%d %H:%M %z'; python3 - <<'EOF'
import math
T=5.5; eps=0.5
drop=lambda t0,D: t0+T*math.log2(D/eps)
def points(intakes):
    start=min(t for t,_ in intakes); end=max(drop(t,D) for t,D in intakes)
    m=math.ceil((end-start)*60)  # first minute sample at/after end -> nothing counts after drop
    return m+1, (end-start)
print("single 200mg:", points([(0,200)]))
day=[(8,128),(10.75,64),(15,205)]
print("one day example:", points(day))
# steady state: same day repeated for 7 days, now = day 6 23:00
intakes=[(d*24+t,D) for d in range(7) for t,D in day]
now=6*24+23
active=[(t,D) for t,D in intakes if drop(t,D)>now and t<=now]
print("steady-state active intakes:", len(active), points(active))
n,_=points(active); print("approx bytes (16/level):", n*16)
EOF
```

approx bytes (16/level): 75616

**21:43:06 · Tool call: Bash** — Timestamp and compute marking times for a ±12 h window

```sh
date '+%Y-%m-%d %H:%M %z'; python3 - <<'EOF'
import math
T=5.5; eps=0.5
for D in (64,128,200,400):
    d=T*math.log2(D/eps); print(f"{D} mg: stops {d:.2f} h, marked {d+12:.2f} h")
t=T*math.log2(200/eps)+12
for TT in (6,7,8): print("T",TT,"200mg at", round(t,2),"h ->", round(200*2**(-t/TT),2))
print("bytes", 1440*16)
EOF
```

- `old_string`:

```
- **`CaffeineDecayRepository` executes the rule.** It fetches the intakes that aren't marked negligible from its data source, applies the rule, and publishes the entire curve on its `AsyncStream`.
- **The repository marks intakes that no longer count.** When the rule returns intakes that are negligible at the current time, the repository has the data source mark them, so later fetches skip them. A mark is state on the intake that the data source stores, and it's never cleared. Marking only saves work. From the current time onward, the curve is the same whether or not an intake has been marked yet.
```
- `new_string`:

```
- **`CaffeineDecayRepository` executes the rule.** It fetches the intakes that aren't marked negligible from its data source, applies the rule for the current time, and publishes the resulting curve on its `AsyncStream`.
- **The curve covers a fixed window.** It runs from 12 hours before the current time to 12 hours after: 1,440 levels, one per minute. The window belongs to the Domain, not to presentation, because the rule decides negligibility at the window's first sample.
- **The repository marks intakes that no longer count.** An intake is negligible once it's past its peak and below 0.5 mg at the window's first sample. When the rule returns such intakes, the repository has the data source mark them, so later fetches skip them. A mark is state on the intake that the data source stores, and it's never cleared. A marked intake adds nothing anywhere in the window, so marking only saves work: the curve is the same whether or not an intake has been marked yet.
```

### 21:46:20 · Claude

The ±12-hour window is now in `CaffeineDecayModel.md`: 1,440 one-minute levels, with an intake marked once it's negligible at the window's first sample. The docs build with no warnings from our catalog. No code changed, so there were no tests to run, and nothing is committed.

**The Domain / presentation split.** I agree the window is a Domain concern: the rule needs the window's first sample to decide when to mark an intake. The split I've documented:
- **Domain (rule and repository):** owns the ±12 h window, the 1,440 levels, and when intakes get marked.
- **Presentation:** chooses what to show within the window, such as just the next few hours, and reads "now" from the middle sample. It never runs the decay math itself.

**What the window changes:**
- **Marking never alters the curve.** An intake is only marked once it's negligible at the window's first sample, and after its peak it only shrinks. So it contributes nothing anywhere in the window by then, and the curve is identical whether or not it's been marked. REPO-4 now holds across the whole window.
- **Marked intakes no longer vanish from the visible past.** That was the earlier problem, and within the window it's gone.
- **Marking happens 12 h after an intake stops counting.** A 200 mg drink is marked at 59.5 h instead of 47.5 h. That also shrinks the error from a later, longer half-life: at 8 h, the leftover is now 1.15 mg rather than 3.25 mg.
- **The size is fixed.** It's always 1,440 levels, about 23 KB, no matter how many drinks are counting.

**Choices I made without asking** (all recorded in the log):
- Samples fall on whole clock minutes, so successive curves line up and tests are exact.
- The window runs from 720 minutes before the current minute to 719 after it. That makes your 1,440 exact, with index 720 as the current minute.
- With no intakes, the curve is 1,440 zeros rather than empty.
- The per-minute 0.5 mg cutoff (RULE-3) still applies inside the window. A tail past its peak and below 0.5 mg counts as 0 even before its intake is marked.

**Decisions for you**
1. **How often should the repository recalculate?** Because "now" moves, the curve goes stale even when no drinks change. I'd recalculate once a minute, matching the sample spacing. That means a timer inside the repository, which tests would drive with a fixed clock.
2. **How does `CaffeineDecayRepository` hear about new intakes, and where does it get the half-life?**

Actor isolation is still deferred. I logged this task in `ai_log.md`, and noted on the previous entry that its sample-alignment choices are superseded.

### 21:47:37 · Human

> Recalculate will be on data refresh and/or view refresh. Since it's a full 24 hour window, there isn't a need to have live updates of 1440 data points per caffeine intake.

### 21:48:11 · Claude

Recalculating on refresh fits the architecture well. A view refresh is simply a new subscription, and a repository already emits a freshly calculated curve to every new subscriber. That means no timer anywhere.

There's one knock-on change. If the curve isn't recalculated each minute, presentation has to find the level for "now" by its date, not at the fixed middle index. Otherwise the "now" figure freezes at the time the curve was calculated. Updating the article:

### 21:49:52 · Claude

I've added all three decisions to `CaffeineDecayModel.md`, and the Architecture article's `CaffeineDecayRepository` row now names both data sources. The docs build with no warnings from our catalog. No code changed, so there were no tests to run, and nothing is committed.

**Recalculating on refresh.** This needed no new mechanism:
- **View refresh:** a view's `.task` starts a new subscription, and the repository already sends every new subscriber a freshly calculated curve.
- **Data refresh:** adding an intake, or a change to the half-life, publishes a new curve to everyone subscribed.
- **No timer:** REPO-2 now states the repository never recalculates on a timer.

**One knock-on requirement (VIEW-3).** Since the curve isn't recalculated every minute, screens find the current level by its date, not at the fixed middle position. Otherwise the "now" figure would freeze at the moment the curve was calculated. A screen can move its "now" marker along the curve it already has, for up to 12 hours.

**New intakes and the half-life.** A use case calls a repository method to add an intake. The repository stores it through the intake data source, then recalculates and publishes. The half-life comes from a half-life data source. I added four requirements for this, and two of them are my additions rather than things you stated, so please check those:

| ID | What it requires | Source |
|---|---|---|
| REPO-5 | Adding an intake stores it, then publishes a new curve | You |
| REPO-6 | The curve uses the stored half-life | You |
| DATA-4 | A new intake is stored unmarked | My addition |
| HALF-1 | With nothing stored, the half-life is the standard 5.5 hours | My addition |

`LogCaffeineIntakeUseCase` is only an example name for now.

**One open question.** Does returning to the foreground count as a view refresh? A view's `.task` doesn't restart when the app comes back from the background. So an app left in memory from 8am until 9pm would still hold the 8am curve, which ends at 8pm. I'd treat foregrounding as a view refresh.

Actor isolation is still deferred. I added this task to `ai_log.md`.

```
1	# Half-Life Constitution
2	
3	> **Status: DRAFT** — starter version for review. Edit, remove, or add principles, then change the status to **Ratified** with the date.
4	
5	This document defines the foundational principles every change to Half-Life must follow. It overrides convenience, existing code patterns, and any other project guidance (including `CLAUDE.md`). If a change can't comply with it, the change is wrong, or this document needs a deliberate amendment. It is never quietly worked around.
6	
7	## Article I: Architecture
8	
9	The app combines **The Composable Architecture (TCA)** ([pointfreeco/swift-composable-architecture](https://github.com/pointfreeco/swift-composable-architecture)) with **Clean Architecture**. It has three layers.
10	
11	| Layer | Contains | May depend on |
12	|-------|----------|---------------|
13	| **Presentation** | TCA features: reducers and their views | Domain (use cases and entities) |
14	| **Domain** | Entities, use cases, and repository protocols | Nothing (Swift and Foundation only) |
15	| **Data** | Repository implementations and data sources (HealthKit, SwiftData, …) | Domain (to implement its protocols) |
16	
17	Dependencies point inward, toward Domain. Domain never imports SwiftUI, ComposableArchitecture, HealthKit, or SwiftData. Nothing depends on Presentation.
18	
19	### Presentation: TCA features
20	
21	1. **Features are reducers.** Each feature is a `@Reducer` with an `@ObservableState` `State`, an `Action` enum, and a `body`. Presentation logic lives in reducers, never in views.
22	2. **Views are thin.** A view renders from a `StoreOf<Feature>` and sends actions. It holds no business state of its own (`@State` only for purely visual, ephemeral concerns).
23	3. **Actions call use cases.** A reducer reaches business logic and data only through use cases, injected with `@Dependency`, and calls them from `Effect`s. It never touches a repository or data source directly.
24	4. **Repository data is reduced into State.** A feature subscribes to repository data through an `Observe…` use case in a `.run` effect. The feature sends one action per emitted value (e.g. `.entriesUpdated([CaffeineEntry])`), and the reducer reduces that value into `State`. The subscription is tied to the feature's lifetime: the view starts it from `.task { await store.send(.task).finish() }`, or it is cancelled by ID when the feature is dismissed.
25	5. **One-way data flow.** Commands flow action → use case → repository. Updates flow repository → `Observe…` use case → action → reducer → `State`. A reducer does not optimistically write repository-owned data into `State`; it waits for the repository to emit. Any documented exception must be recorded in the DocC catalog.
26	6. **Composition and navigation.** Features compose with `Scope`, `.ifLet`, and `.forEach`. Navigation is state-driven using `@Presents` / `PresentationAction` and `StackState` / `StackAction`.
27	
28	### Domain: use cases and entities
29	
30	7. **Use cases are single-purpose.** Each use case performs exactly one business operation, exposed as `callAsFunction`. It is named for that operation: `LogCaffeineIntakeUseCase`, `ObserveCaffeineEntriesUseCase`.
31	8. **Use cases are lifetime-scoped.** A use case lives only as long as the feature that owns it. It holds no state of its own beyond references to the repositories it uses; long-lived state belongs in repositories, and per-screen state belongs in feature `State`. Use cases never cache data between calls.
32	9. **Use cases depend on repository protocols**, which Domain defines. They never depend on concrete implementations.
33	10. **Entities** are plain `Sendable`, `Equatable` value types with no framework dependencies.
34	
35	### Data: repositories and data sources
36	
37	11. **Repositories hold state.** A repository is the single source of truth for its data. It is app-scoped, created once, and outlives every feature. It holds the current data in memory and persists it through data sources.
38	12. **Repositories publish changes.** Every repository exposes its data as an `AsyncStream` that emits the current value immediately on subscription and then every change.
39	13. **Repositories are concurrency-safe:** an `actor`, or isolated to a single global actor.
40	14. **Data sources wrap frameworks.** Data sources are the only code that touches HealthKit, SwiftData, the file system, or other system frameworks. Repositories talk to data sources, never to frameworks directly.
```

```
1	# Half-Life
2	
3	iOS caffeine-tracking app built with SwiftUI, The Composable Architecture (TCA), and Clean Architecture (Xcode 26, iOS 26.5 deployment target, Swift default actor isolation = `MainActor`).
4	
5	Every change to this repository must comply with the three governing documents below. They are not suggestions — a change that violates any of them is not done.
6	
7	## 1. Constitution — `constitution.md`
8	
9	@constitution.md
10	
11	- `constitution.md` defines the foundational principles all code in this repo follows: architecture, dependencies, privacy, accessibility, localization, documentation, code style, and logging. Read it before starting any change.
12	- It takes precedence over everything else in this file, over existing code patterns, and over convenience. If a request conflicts with it, stop and raise the conflict instead of working around it.
13	- Where it leaves something undecided, ask rather than choosing.
14	- Only change `constitution.md` itself when explicitly asked.
15	
16	### Architecture at a glance (constitution Article I is authoritative)
17	
18	- **Presentation → Domain ← Data.** Domain imports nothing but Swift and Foundation.
19	- **Reducers call use cases**, never repositories or data sources.
20	- **Use cases** are single-purpose (`<Verb><Noun>UseCase`, one `callAsFunction`) and lifetime-scoped to their feature. They hold no state.
21	- **Repositories** are app-scoped, stateful sources of truth that expose an `AsyncStream` of their data.
22	- **Features observe** repository data through `Observe…UseCase` in a `.run` effect started from `.task { await store.send(.task).finish() }`, and reduce each emitted value into `State`. Don't write repository-owned data into `State` optimistically.
23	- **Only data sources** touch HealthKit, SwiftData, or other system frameworks.
24	- The DocC catalog's `Architecture` article shows the folder layout and patterns. Keep it current in the same change.
25	
26	### Open issue: actor isolation vs. TCA (undecided, don't guess)
27	
28	The app target sets `SWIFT_DEFAULT_ACTOR_ISOLATION = MainActor`, and TCA does not work cleanly with it. This was verified on 2026-09-11 against TCA 1.26.2 in a scratch copy of the project:
29	
30	- **As-is:** reducers crash at runtime with infinite `reduce`/`_reduce` recursion.
31	- **Marking reducers and their `State`/`Action` `nonisolated`:** tests pass, but TCA's macro-generated conformances produce warnings, which become errors in Swift 6 mode. That violates Article IX.
32	- **Switching the app target to a `nonisolated` default:** builds with zero warnings, and tests pass.
33	
34	The user has deferred this decision. **Do not write the first TCA feature until the user settles it.** Raise it as soon as TCA work is requested.
35	
36	## 2. AI interaction log — `ai_log.md`
37	
38	- `ai_log.md` is the timestamped log of **every** AI interaction on this project. Questions, reviews, debugging, and planning count too, not just changes. Its **Rules** section defines the format; read it before finishing any task.
39	- Log every task, including ones that change no files. A task is not finished until its entry is appended, and for a change the entry goes in the same commit.
40	- Take every timestamp from the clock with `date '+%Y-%m-%d %H:%M %z'`. Run it when you receive the prompt that starts a task, at each later prompt or decision, and when the task ends. Never estimate a time afterwards; if one must be reconstructed, mark it `~` and say from what.
```

import CoreGraphics

/// Spacing between and inside elements, in points. Every value sits on a 4 pt grid.
///
/// See the Design System article for where each value comes from.
nonisolated enum Spacing {
    /// The inset between the screen's leading and trailing edges and its content.
    static let screenMargin: CGFloat = 20
    /// The inset inside large cards.
    static let cardPadding: CGFloat = 20
    /// The inset inside tiles and other small cards.
    static let cardPaddingCompact: CGFloat = 16
    /// The space between stacked cards.
    static let cardGap: CGFloat = 16
    /// The space between chips, tiles, and options in a group.
    static let itemGap: CGFloat = 12
    /// The space between sections of a screen.
    static let sectionGap: CGFloat = 28
    /// The space between a section's label and its content.
    static let sectionHeaderGap: CGFloat = 12
}

import Foundation
import Testing

@testable import Half_Life

/// Checks the half-life entity against the Caffeine Decay Model article's Constants and Entities sections.
struct CaffeineHalfLifeTests {

    @Test func standardIsFiveAndAHalfHours() {
        #expect(CaffeineHalfLife.standard.seconds == 19_800)
    }

    @Test func acceptsPositiveFiniteSeconds() throws {
        let halfLife = try #require(CaffeineHalfLife(seconds: 3_600))
        #expect(halfLife.seconds == 3_600)
    }

    @Test(arguments: [0, -1, -19_800, .nan, .infinity, -.infinity] as [TimeInterval])
    func rejectsSecondsThatArentPositiveAndFinite(_ seconds: TimeInterval) {
        #expect(CaffeineHalfLife(seconds: seconds) == nil)
    }
}

//  _______  __   __  ___   ___      ___      _______  __    _  _______
// |       ||  | |  ||   | |   |    |   |    |   _   ||  |  | ||       |
// |   _   ||  | |  ||   | |   |    |   |    |  |_|  ||   |_| ||   _   |
// |  | |  ||  |_|  ||   | |   |    |   |    |       ||       ||  | |  |
// |  |_|  ||       ||   | |   |___ |   |___ |       ||  _    ||  |_|  |
// |      | |       ||   | |       ||       ||   _   || | |   ||      |
// |____||_||_______||___| |_______||_______||__| |__||_|  |__||____||_|
//
// Half-Life CaffeineHalfLife
//

import Foundation

/// A person's caffeine elimination half-life: the time their body takes to clear half the caffeine present.
///
/// The half-life is the decay model's only per-user parameter. It's always positive and finite, so a value of
/// this type is always one the model can use. See the Caffeine Decay Model article.
nonisolated struct CaffeineHalfLife: Sendable, Equatable {
    /// The standard half-life of 5.5 hours, used until the user's own half-life is known.
    static let standard = CaffeineHalfLife(validSeconds: 19_800)

    /// The half-life, in seconds. Always positive and finite.
    let seconds: TimeInterval

    /// Creates a half-life, or returns `nil` if `seconds` isn't positive and finite.
    ///
    /// - Parameter seconds: The half-life, in seconds.
    init?(seconds: TimeInterval) {
        guard seconds > 0, seconds.isFinite else { return nil }
        self.seconds = seconds
    }

    private init(validSeconds: TimeInterval) {
        seconds = validSeconds
    }
}

```

//  _______  __   __  ___   ___      ___      _______  __    _  _______
// |       ||  | |  ||   | |   |    |   |    |   _   ||  |  | ||       |
// |   _   ||  | |  ||   | |   |    |   |    |  |_|  ||   |_| ||   _   |
// |  | |  ||  |_|  ||   | |   |    |   |    |       ||       ||  | |  |
// |  |_|  ||       ||   | |   |___ |   |___ |       ||  _    ||  |_|  |
// |      | |       ||   | |       ||       ||   _   || | |   ||      |
// |____||_||_______||___| |_______||_______||__| |__||_|  |__||____||_|
//
// Half-LifeTests CaffeineDecayRuleTests
//

import Foundation
import Testing

@testable import Half_Life

/// Checks the decay rule against the Caffeine Decay Model article: requirements RULE-1 to RULE-7, and the worked
/// examples, which are the reference values for RULE-1.
struct CaffeineDecayRuleTests {

    let rule = CaffeineDecayRule()
    let halfLife = CaffeineHalfLife.standard

    /// Midnight on the day of the worked examples. It sits on a whole minute, like every sample.
    static let midnight = Date(timeIntervalSinceReferenceDate: 0)

    /// The worked examples' day of drinks: 128 mg at 8:00am, 64 mg at 10:45am, and 205 mg at 3:00pm.
    static let dayOfDrinks = [intake(128, hours: 8), intake(64, hours: 10.75), intake(205, hours: 15)]

    /// The date `hours` after midnight.
    static func date(_ hours: Double) -> Date {
        midnight.addingTimeInterval(hours * 3_600)
    }

    static func intake(_ milligrams: Double, hours: Double) -> CaffeineIntake {
        CaffeineIntake(id: UUID(), milligrams: milligrams, consumedAt: date(hours))
    }

    /// Rounds to two decimal places, the precision the model is specified to.
    static func rounded(_ milligrams: Double) -> Double {
        (milligrams * 100).rounded() / 100
    }

    @Test func constantsMatchTheArticle() {
        #expect(CaffeineDecayRule.negligibleMilligrams == 0.5)
        #expect(CaffeineDecayRule.sampleSpacing == 60)
        #expect(CaffeineDecayRule.windowRadius == 12 * 3_600)
        #expect(CaffeineDecayRule.sampleCount == 1_440)
    }

    // MARK: - RULE-1: the level is the sum of each counting intake's decay

    @Test(
        arguments: [
            (0, 200.00), (1, 176.32), (2, 155.44), (4, 120.81), (5.5, 100.00), (7, 82.78), (11, 50.00),
            (16.5, 25.00), (24, 9.72),
        ] as [(Double, Double)])
    func singleDrinkMatchesWorkedExample(hoursSinceDrinking: Double, expected: Double) {
        let level = rule.level(
            at: Self.date(hoursSinceDrinking), from: [Self.intake(200, hours: 0)], halfLife: halfLife)
        #expect(level.date == Self.date(hoursSinceDrinking))
        #expect(Self.rounded(level.milligrams) == expected)
    }

    @Test(
        arguments: [
            (7 + 59.0 / 60, 0.00), (8, 128.00), (10.75, 154.51), (12, 131.99), (15, 295.44), (23, 107.80),
        ] as [(Double, Double)])
    func dayOfDrinksMatchesWorkedExample(hours: Double, expected: Double) {
        let level = rule.level(at: Self.date(hours), from: Self.dayOfDrinks, halfLife: halfLife)
        #expect(Self.rounded(level.milligrams) == expected)
    }

    // MARK: - RULE-2: nothing before consumedAt, the full dose at consumedAt

    @Test func intakeAddsNothingBeforeItsConsumedAt() {
        let level = rule.level(
            at: Self.date(8).addingTimeInterval(-1), from: [Self.intake(128, hours: 8)], halfLife: halfLife)
        #expect(level.milligrams == 0)
    }

    @Test func intakeAddsItsFullDoseAtExactlyItsConsumedAt() {
        let level = rule.level(at: Self.date(8), from: [Self.intake(128, hours: 8)], halfLife: halfLife)
        #expect(level.milligrams == 128)
    }

    // MARK: - RULE-3: an intake counts while at least 0.5 mg remains

    /// 128 mg is exactly 0.5 mg after 44 hours (8 half-lives).
    @Test func intakeStillCountsAtExactlyHalfAMilligram() {
        let level = rule.level(at: Self.date(44), from: [Self.intake(128, hours: 0)], halfLife: halfLife)
        #expect(level.milligrams == 0.5)
    }

    @Test func intakeStopsCountingOnceBelowHalfAMilligram() {
        let level = rule.level(
            at: Self.date(44).addingTimeInterval(1), from: [Self.intake(128, hours: 0)], halfLife: halfLife)
        #expect(level.milligrams == 0)
    }

    @Test func intakeOfLessThanHalfAMilligramNeverCounts() {
        let level = rule.level(at: Self.date(0), from: [Self.intake(0.4, hours: 0)], halfLife: halfLife)
        #expect(level.milligrams == 0)
    }

    // MARK: - RULE-4: negligible intakes are judged at the window's first sample

    /// At 56 hours plus a minute, the first sample is 44 hours plus a minute, when 128 mg is below 0.5 mg.
    @Test func intakeIsNegligibleOnceItStopsCountingAtTheFirstSample() {
        let intake = Self.intake(128, hours: 0)
        let negligible = rule.negligibleIntakes(
            from: [intake], halfLife: halfLife, now: Self.date(56).addingTimeInterval(60))
        #expect(negligible == [intake])
    }

    /// At 56 hours, the first sample is 44 hours, when 128 mg is exactly 0.5 mg and still counts.
    @Test func intakeIsntNegligibleWhileItCountsAtTheFirstSample() {
        let negligible = rule.negligibleIntakes(
            from: [Self.intake(128, hours: 0)], halfLife: halfLife, now: Self.date(56))
        #expect(negligible.isEmpty)
    }

    /// 0.4 mg never counts, but it isn't past its peak until the first sample reaches its consumedAt.
    @Test func intakeConsumedAfterTheFirstSampleIsNeverNegligible() {
        let negligible = rule.negligibleIntakes(
            from: [Self.intake(0.4, hours: 10)], halfLife: halfLife, now: Self.date(12))
        #expect(negligible.isEmpty)
    }

    /// 64 mg stops counting after 38.5 hours. At 50.5 hours plus a minute, the first sample is just past that.
    @Test func returnsExactlyTheNegligibleIntakes() {
        let old = Self.intake(64, hours: 0)
        let recent = Self.intake(200, hours: 40)
        let negligible = rule.negligibleIntakes(
            from: [old, recent], halfLife: halfLife, now: Self.date(50.5).addingTimeInterval(60))
        #expect(negligible == [old])
    }

    // MARK: - RULE-5: 1,440 levels, 12 hours either side of the current minute

    @Test func curveHasFourteenHundredFortyLevels() {
        let curve = rule.curve(from: Self.dayOfDrinks, halfLife: halfLife, now: Self.date(12))
        #expect(curve.count == 1_440)
    }

    /// At 12:00:59, the current minute is 12:00, so the window runs from midnight.
    @Test func curveStartsTwelveHoursBeforeTheCurrentMinute() {
        let curve = rule.curve(
            from: Self.dayOfDrinks, halfLife: halfLife, now: Self.date(12).addingTimeInterval(59))
        #expect(curve.first?.date == Self.date(0))
        #expect(curve[720].date == Self.date(12))
        #expect(curve.last?.date == Self.date(24).addingTimeInterval(-60))
    }

    @Test func curveWithNoIntakesIsAllZeros() {
        let curve = rule.curve(from: [], halfLife: halfLife, now: Self.date(12))
        #expect(curve.count == 1_440)
        #expect(curve.allSatisfy { $0.milligrams == 0 })
    }

    @Test func eachCurveLevelIsTheLevelAtItsDate() {
        let curve = rule.curve(from: Self.dayOfDrinks, halfLife: halfLife, now: Self.date(20))
        #expect(curve.allSatisfy { $0 == rule.level(at: $0.date, from: Self.dayOfDrinks, halfLife: halfLife) })
    }

    /// At 8:00pm the window runs from 8:00am, so 3:00pm is sample 420 and 11:00pm is sample 900.
    @Test func curveContainsTheWorkedExamples() {
        let curve = rule.curve(from: Self.dayOfDrinks, halfLife: halfLife, now: Self.date(20))
        #expect(Self.rounded(curve[420].milligrams) == 295.44)
        #expect(Self.rounded(curve[900].milligrams) == 107.80)
    }

    // MARK: - RULE-6: pure, with the current time as an input

    @Test func sameInputsGiveTheSameCurve() {
        let first = rule.curve(from: Self.dayOfDrinks, halfLife: halfLife, now: Self.date(20))
        let second = rule.curve(from: Self.dayOfDrinks, halfLife: halfLife, now: Self.date(20))
        #expect(first == second)
    }

    // MARK: - RULE-7: one minute apart, on whole clock minutes

    @Test func curveSamplesAreOneMinuteApartOnWholeMinutes() {
        let curve = rule.curve(
            from: Self.dayOfDrinks, halfLife: halfLife, now: Self.date(12).addingTimeInterval(37))
        let gaps = zip(curve, curve.dropFirst()).map { $1.date.timeIntervalSince($0.date) }
        #expect(gaps.allSatisfy { $0 == 60 })
        #expect(curve.allSatisfy { $0.date.timeIntervalSinceReferenceDate.truncatingRemainder(dividingBy: 60) == 0 })
    }
}

```

import Foundation

/// One drink's caffeine entering the body: the dose, and the moment it was consumed.
///
/// An intake holds only what the decay model needs. A drink's name, size, and other details belong to the drink
/// composer's own types. See the Caffeine Decay Model article.
nonisolated struct CaffeineIntake: Identifiable, Sendable, Equatable {
    /// Identifies the intake, so it can be listed, edited, deleted, or marked negligible.
    let id: UUID
    /// The caffeine in the drink, in milligrams.
    let milligrams: Double
    /// When the drink was consumed. It can be earlier than when it was logged.
    let consumedAt: Date
}
